This is a combinatorial problem related to packing sets with size constraints on their intersections.\n\nFirst, note that A has 10 elements. The problem restricts us to non-empty subsets and intersection size at most 2. One possible strategy is to consider how many subsets we can have with pairwise intersections of size at most 2. To maximize k, we might try to construct subsets in a way that minimizes overlaps beyond two elements. However, directly constructing such a family might be complex, so another approach is to use known combinatorial designs or inequalities like the Erdos-Ko-Rado theorem, though that theorem applies to intersecting families, which this is not. Instead, we can consider a greedy algorithm or counting arguments.\n\nAlternatively, we can think about the problem using the concept of "packing." In packing problems, we often try to partition the ground set into smaller subsets (in this case, pairs and singletons) and then build the maximum family around those. However, since the intersection of any two subsets can have at most two elements, we cannot have three subsets that all intersect each other in the same two elements. Therefore, we need to ensure that any two subsets intersect in at most two elements. \n\nAnother approach is to model this as a graph problem where each subset is a node, and edges connect nodes that have an intersection larger than two.
